I've always been much more on the quiet, daydreaming side.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems cause difficulties in more than one area of your life, such as at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely affect both work and home. At work, I'm falling behind on reports and feel like my professional credibility is slipping despite working longer hours. It also spills over at home, where I'm constantly having to tighten my routines just to keep up and frequently misplace everyday items around the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of hyperactivity or impulsivity, such as feeling restless, fidgeting, interrupting others, or acting before thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those symptoms at all. I don't feel restless, fidgety, or prone to interrupting others. My struggles are strictly centered around focus, attention, and organization.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention and organization problems continue without a long symptom-free period from childhood through adolescence into adulthood, or did they mostly start later in life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have been lifelong and continuous without any real symptom-free period. I've always struggled with focus, but I managed to compensate for years into adulthood with intense over-preparation, color-coded systems, and working late hours. It was only when my recent promotion dramatically increased my workload that those coping strategies finally broke down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had significant hyperactivity or impulsive symptoms, such as feeling unable to sit still, fidgeting, blurting things out, or interrupting others, especially since childhood?\n\nPatient: No, I have never really experienced those hyperactive or impulsive symptoms, even as a child. Back then, my struggles were actually the opposite—I was very quiet and tended to daydream or get lost in my thoughts rather than being physically restless. I was always more likely to miss instructions because my mind drifted, not because I couldn't sit still."

However, these focus issues aren't entirely new; even back in elementary school, my teachers noted that I daydreamed a lot, and my parents had to sit with me for hours just to keep me on track with my homework.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have similar problems with inattention, forgetfulness, or being easily distracted at school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Even back in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was smart but \"daydreamed\" and missed instructions. At home, my parents had to sit with me at the kitchen table for hours because I’d constantly lose track of what I was supposed to be doing on my homework.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of hyperactivity or impulsivity, such as feeling restless, fidgeting, interrupting others, or acting before thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of symptoms. My struggles are almost entirely related to focus, attention, and keeping track of things, rather than feeling physically restless or acting impulsively. I've always been much more on the quiet, daydreaming side.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems cause difficulties in more than one area of your life, such as at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely affect both work and home. At work, I'm falling behind on reports and feel like my professional credibility is slipping despite working longer hours. It also spills over at home, where I'm constantly having to tighten my routines just to keep up and frequently misplace everyday items around the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of hyperactivity or impulsivity, such as feeling restless, fidgeting, interrupting others, or acting before thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those symptoms at all. I don't feel restless, fidgety, or prone to interrupting others. My struggles are strictly centered around focus, attention, and organization.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention and organization problems continue without a long symptom-free period from childhood through adolescence into adulthood, or did they mostly start later in life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have been lifelong and continuous without any real symptom-free period. I've always struggled with focus, but I managed to compensate for years into adulthood with intense over-preparation, color-coded systems, and working late hours. It was only when my recent promotion dramatically increased my workload that those coping strategies finally broke down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had significant hyperactivity or impulsive symptoms, such as feeling unable to sit still, fidgeting, blurting things out, or interrupting others, especially since childhood?\n\nPatient: No, I have never really experienced those hyperactive or impulsive symptoms, even as a child. Back then, my struggles were actually the opposite—I was very quiet and tended to daydream or get lost in my thoughts rather than being physically restless. I was always more likely to miss instructions because my mind drifted, not because I couldn't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and organization problems caused clear problems in at least two settings, such as work, home, or school, and did they begin before age 12?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely started before age 12, as my elementary school teachers noted I daydreamed and missed instructions, and my parents had to sit with me for hours just to get homework done. Today, it severely impacts both settings; I'm working exhausting hours at my job to avoid falling further behind, and at home, I struggle to maintain routines and constantly misplace everyday items like my keys and work badge."

Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).
